EXHIBIT 3.1 Sample size and posterior trade-off for the normal mean parameter


In the next chapter, we discuss the univariate and multivariate linear regression models, which, under the assumptions of normality of the regression disturbances and conjugate priors, are straightforward extensions of this chapter’s framework. 

## APPENDIX: DEFINITIONS OF SOME UNIVARIATE AND MULTIVARIATE STATISTICAL DISTRIBUTIONS

Here we review some statistical distributions commonly used in Bayesian financial applications. Other distributions are defined in the chapters where they are mentioned. See, for example, Chapter 13 for an overview of several heavy-tailed and asymmetric distributions that have been employed in the empirical finance literature to model asset returns.<sup>25</sup> 

## The Univariate Normal Distribution

A random variable, $X , - \infty < x < \infty$ , distributed with the normal (also called Gaussian) distribution with mean $\mu$ and variance $\sigma ^ { 2 }$ , has the density function 

$$
f (x \mid \mu , \sigma^ {2}) = \frac {1}{\sqrt {2 \pi} \sigma} e ^ {- \frac {(x - \mu) ^ {2}}{2 \sigma^ {2}}},\tag{3.25}
$$

where $- \infty < \mu < \infty$ and $\sigma > 0$ . The standard deviation, $\sigma ,$ , is the scale of the normal distribution. We denote the distribution by N $\left( \mu , \sigma ^ { 2 } \right)$ 

## The Univariate Student’s t-Distribution

A random variable $X , \ - \infty \ < \ x \ < \ \infty$ , distributed with the Student’s t-distribution with ν degrees of freedom, has the density function 

$$
f (x \mid \nu , \mu , \sigma) = \frac {\Gamma (\frac {\nu + 1}{2})}{\sigma \Gamma (\frac {\nu}{2}) \sqrt {\nu \pi}} \left(1 + \frac {1}{\nu} \left(\frac {x - \mu}{\sigma}\right) ^ {2}\right) ^ {- (\nu + 1) / 2},\tag{3.26}
$$

where  is the Gamma functio $\downarrow , - \infty < \mu < \infty$ is the mode of X and $\sigma > 0$ is the scale parameter of X. We denote this distribution by $t ( \nu , \mu , \sigma )$ . The mean and variance of X are given, respectively, by 

$$
\operatorname{E} (X) = \mu
$$

and 

$$
\operatorname{var} (X) = \frac {\nu}{\nu - 2} \sigma^ {2}.\tag{3.27}
$$

The variance exists for values of ν greater than 2 and the mean—for ν greater than 1. 

## The Inverted $\chi ^ { 2 }$ Distribution

A random variable $X , x > 0 ,$ , distributed with the inverted $\chi ^ { 2 }$ distribution with ν degrees of freedom and scale parameter $^ { c , }$ has the following density 

function, 

$$
f (x \mid \nu , c) = \frac {1}{\Gamma \left(\frac {\nu}{2}\right)} \left(\frac {\nu}{2}\right) ^ {\nu / 2} c ^ {\nu} x ^ {- (\frac {\nu}{2} + 1)} \exp \left(- \frac {\nu c}{2 x}\right),\tag{3.28}
$$

where $\nu > 0 , c > 0$ , and $x > 0$ . The inverted $\chi ^ { 2 }$ distribution is denoted as In $\operatorname { v } - \chi ^ { 2 } ( \nu , c )$ . Its kernel consists of the nonconstant part of the density function, 

$$
x ^ {- (\frac {\nu}{2} + 1)} \exp \left(- \frac {\nu c}{2 x}\right).
$$

The inverted $\chi ^ { 2 }$ distribution is a particular case of the inverted gamma distribution, 

$$
\mathrm{Inv-} \chi^ {2} (\nu , c) \equiv \mathrm{IG} \left(\frac {\nu}{2}, \frac {\nu c}{2}\right).
$$

The mean (defined for $\nu > 2 )$ ) and the variance (defined for $\nu > 4 )$ of X are given, respectively, by 

$$
\operatorname{E} (X) = \frac {\nu}{\nu - 2} c
$$

and 

$$
\operatorname{var} (X) = \frac {2 \nu^ {2}}{(\nu - 2) ^ {2} (\nu - 4)} c ^ {2}.\tag{3.29}
$$

## The Multivariate Normal Distribution

An $n \times 1$ vector ${ \mathfrak { x } } = ( x _ { 1 } , \ x _ { 2 } , \ \ldots , \ x _ { n } ) ^ { \prime }$ , distributed with the multivariate normal distribution, has a density 

$$
f (x \mid \boldsymbol {\mu}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) = (2 \pi) ^ {n / 2} | \boldsymbol {\Sigma} | ^ {- 1 / 2} \exp \left(- \frac {1}{2} (x - \boldsymbol {\mu}) ^ {\prime} \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {- 1} (x - \boldsymbol {\mu})\right),\tag{3.30}
$$

where the $n \times 1$ vector of means is $\pmb { \mu } = ( \mu _ { 1 } , \ \mu _ { 2 } , \ . . . , \ \mu _ { n } ) ^ { \prime }$ and the $n \times n$ matrix - is the (positive semidefinite) covariance matrix. The diagonal elements of - are the variances of each of the components of x, while the off-diagonal elements are the covariances, cov $( x _ { i } , x _ { j } ) , i \neq j ,$ , between each two components of x. Since $\operatorname { c o v } ( x _ { i } , x _ { j } )$ is the same as $\operatorname { c o v } ( x _ { j } , x _ { i } )$ , - is symmetric and contains $n ( n - 1 ) / 2$ distinct elements. 

## The Multivariate Student’s t-Distribution

An $n \times 1$ vector ${ \mathfrak { x } } = ( x _ { 1 } , \ x _ { 2 } , \ \ldots , \ x _ { n } ) ^ { \prime }$ , distributed with the multivariate (scaled, non-central) Student’s t-distribution, has the density 

$$
f (x \mid \nu , \mu , S) = C \times \left(\nu + (x - \mu) ^ {\prime} S (x - \mu)\right) ^ {- (n + \nu) / 2},
$$

where $\begin{array} { r } { C = \frac { \nu ^ { \nu / 2 } \Gamma ( ( \nu + n ) / 2 ) | S | ^ { 1 / 2 } } { \pi ^ { n / 2 } \Gamma ( \nu / 2 ) } } \end{array}$ , ν is the degrees-of-freedom parameter, regulating the tail thickness, $\pmb { \mu }$ is the mean vector, and S is the scale matrix. We denote the distribution by $t ( \nu , \mu , S )$ . The covariance matrix of x is given by 

$$
\pmb {\Sigma} = S ^ {- 1} \frac {\nu}{\nu - 2}.\tag{3.31}
$$

The covariance matrix exists for $\nu > 2$ , and the mean—for $\nu > 1$ 

## The Wishart Distribution

Suppose we have observed a sample of $N \times 1$ vectors, $X _ { 1 } , . . . , X _ { t } , . . . , X _ { T }$ The vectors are independently distributed with multivariate normal distribu tion, $\mathrm { N } ( { \pmb \mu } , { \pmb \Sigma } )$ . The Wishart distribution arises in statistics as the distribution of the quantity, $\mathcal { Q } ,$ 

$$
Q = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {T} \left(X _ {t} - \overline {{X}}\right) \left(X _ {t} - \overline {{X}}\right) ^ {\prime},
$$

which is equal to T times the sample covariance matrix and $\begin{array} { r } { \overline { { \boldsymbol X } } = \frac { 1 } { T } \sum _ { t = 1 } ^ { T } \boldsymbol X _ { t } } \end{array}$ If Q is a positive definite matrix, its density function is given by 

$$
f (\boldsymbol {Q} \mid T, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) = \frac {| \boldsymbol {Q} | ^ {\frac {1}{2} (T - N - 1)} \exp \left(- \frac {1}{2} \operatorname{tr} \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {Q}\right)}{2 ^ {N T / 2} \pi^ {N (N - 1) / 4} | \boldsymbol {\Sigma} | ^ {T / 2} \prod_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \Gamma ((T + 1 - i) / 2)}.\tag{3.32}
$$

The Wishart distribution is denoted by $\mathbb { W } ( T - 1 , \ \pmb { \Sigma } )$ 

## The Inverted Wishart Distribution

In the Bayesian framework, the inverted Wishart distribution is the conjugate prior distribution of the normal covariance matrix. Consider the positive definite matrix $\mathcal { Q }$ above. Denote by S its inverse, $S = Q ^ { - 1 }$ . Its density is given by 

$$
f (S \mid \Psi , \nu) = \frac {| \Psi | ^ {\nu / 2}}{2 ^ {\nu N / 2} \pi^ {N (N - 1) / 4} \prod_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \Gamma (\nu - i + 1) / 2} \frac {\exp \left(- \frac {1}{2} \mathrm{tr} S ^ {- 1} \Psi\right)}{| S | ^ {(\nu + n + 1) / 2}},\tag{3.33}
$$

where $\pmb { \Psi } = \pmb { \Sigma } ^ { - 1 }$ and ν is a (scalar) degrees-of-freedom parameter, such that $\nu \geq N$ . We denote the distribution above as $\operatorname { I W } ( \Psi , \ \nu )$ 26 

The inverted Wishart distribution is a generalization of the inverted gamma distribution to the multivariate case. The diagonal elements of S have the inverted $\chi ^ { 2 }$ distribution in (3.28). 

$$
\operatorname{E} (S) = \Psi \frac {1}{N - T - 1}.
$$

The expectation of an inverted Wishart random variable is 

(3.34) 

# Bayesian Linear Regression Model

egression analysis is one of the most common econometric tools R <sub>employed in the area of investment management. Since the follow-</sub> ing chapters rely on it in the discussion of various financial applications, here we review the Bayesian approach to estimation of the univariate and multivariate regression models. 

## THE UNIVARIATE LINEAR REGRESSION MODEL

The univariate linear regression model attempts to explain the variability in one variable (called the dependent variable) with the help of one or more other variables (called explanatory or independent variables) by asserting a linear relationship between them. We write the model as 

$$
Y = \alpha + \beta_ {1} X _ {1} + \beta_ {2} X _ {2} + \dots \beta_ {K - 1} X _ {K - 1} + \epsilon ,\tag{4.1}
$$

where: Y = dependent variable. 

X = independent (explanatory) variables, $k = 1 , . . . , K - 1$ 

α = regression intercept. 

β = regression (slope) coefficients, $k = 1 , . . . , K - 1$ , representing the effect a unit change in $X _ { k } , k = 1 , . . . , K - 1$ , has on Y, keeping the remaining independent variables, $X _ { j } , j \neq k ,$ , fixed. 

 = regression disturbance. 

The regression disturbance is the source of randomness about the lin ear (deterministic) relationship between the dependent and independent variables. Whereas $\alpha + \beta _ { 1 } X _ { 1 } + \cdot \cdot \cdot + \beta _ { K - 1 } X _ { K - 1 }$ represents the part of $Y ^ { \bullet } s$ variability explained by $X _ { k } , k = 1 , \ldots , K - 1 , \epsilon$ represents the variability in Y left unexplained.<sup>1</sup> 

Suppose that we have n observations of the dependent and the independent variables available. These data are then described by 

$$
y _ {i} = \alpha + \beta_ {1} x _ {1, i} + \dots + \beta_ {K - 1} x _ {K - 1, i} + \epsilon_ {i} \quad i = 1, \dots , n.\tag{4.2}
$$

The subscript $i , i = 1 , \ldots , n ,$ refers to the ith observation of the respective random variable. To describe the source of randomness, $\epsilon ,$ one needs to make a distributional assumption about it. For simplicity, assume that $\epsilon _ { i } , \ i \ = \ 1 , . . . , \ n .$ , are independently and identically distributed (i.i.d.) with the normal distribution and have zero means and (equal) variances, $\sigma ^ { 2 }$ . Then, the dependent variable, $\boldsymbol { Y } ,$ has a normal distribution as well, 

$$
y _ {i} \sim \mathrm{N} (\mu_ {i}, \sigma^ {2}),\tag{4.3}
$$

where $\mu _ { i } = \alpha + \beta _ { 1 } x _ { 1 , i } + \cdot \cdot \cdot + \beta _ { K - 1 } x _ { K - 1 , i } .$ . Notice that the constant-variance assumption in (4.3) is quite restrictive. We come back to this issue later in the chapter. 

The expression in (4.2) is often written in the following compact form: 

$$
y = X \boldsymbol {\beta} + \epsilon ,\tag{4.4}
$$

where y is a $n \times 1$ vector, 

$$
y = \left( \begin{array}{c} y _ {1} \\ y _ {2} \\ \vdots \\ y _ {n} \end{array} \right),
$$

$\beta$ is a $( K ) \times 1$ vector, 

$$
\boldsymbol {\beta} = \left( \begin{array}{c} \alpha \\ \beta_ {1} \\ \vdots \\ \beta_ {K - 1} \end{array} \right),
$$

X is ${ \mathfrak { a } } ~ n \times ( K )$ matrix whose first column consists of ones, 

$$
X = \left( \begin{array}{c c c c} 1 & x _ {1, 1} & \dots & x _ {K - 1, 1} \\ 1 & x _ {1, 2} & \dots & x _ {K - 1, 2} \\ \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \vdots \\ 1 & x _ {1, n} & \dots & x _ {K - 1, n} \end{array} \right),
$$

and $\epsilon$ is a $n \times 1$ vector, 

$$
\boldsymbol {\epsilon} = \left( \begin{array}{c} \epsilon_ {1} \\ \epsilon_ {2} \\ \vdots \\ \epsilon_ {n} \end{array} \right).
$$

We write the normal distributional assumption for the regression disturbances in compact form as 

$$
\epsilon \sim \mathrm{N} (0, \sigma^ {2} I _ {n}),
$$

where $I _ { n }$ is a $( n \times n )$ identity matrix. The parameters in (4.4) we need to estimate are $\hat { \beta }$ and $\sigma ^ { 2 }$ . Assuming normally distributed disturbances, we write the likelihood function for the model parameters as 

$$
\begin{array}{l} L (\alpha , \beta_ {1}, \beta_ {2}, \sigma \mid y, X) = (2 \pi \sigma^ {2}) ^ {- n / 2} \\ \qquad \times \exp \left\{- \frac {1}{2 \sigma^ {2}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} (y _ {i} - \alpha - \beta_ {1} x _ {1, i} - \dots - \beta_ {K - 1} x _ {K - 1, i}) ^ {2} \right\}. \end{array}
$$

$\mathrm { O r } ,$ in vector notation, we have the likelihood function for the parameters of a multivariate normal distribution, 

$$
L (\boldsymbol {\beta}, \sigma \mid y, X) = (2 \pi \sigma^ {2}) ^ {- n / 2} \exp \left\{- \frac {1}{2 \sigma^ {2}} (y - X \boldsymbol {\beta}) ^ {\prime} (y - X \boldsymbol {\beta}) \right\}.\tag{4.5}
$$

## Bayesian Estimation of the Univariate Regression Model

In the classical setting, the regression parameters are usually estimated by maximizing the model’s likelihood with respect to $\widehat { \beta }$ and $\sigma ^ { 2 } { \mathrm { . } }$ , for instance, the likelihood in (4.5) if the normal distribution is assumed. When disturbances are assumed to be normally distributed, the maximum likelihood and the ordinary least squares (OLS) methods produce identical parameter estimates. It can be shown that the OLS estimator of the regression coefficients vector, ${ \widehat { \beta } } ,$ , is given by 

$$
\widehat {\pmb {\beta}} = (X ^ {\prime} X) ^ {- 1} X y,\tag{4.6}
$$

where the prime symbol (<sup></sup>) denotes a matrix transpose.<sup>2</sup> The estimator of $\sigma ^ { 2 } \mathrm {  i } \mathrm { s } ^ { 3 }$ 

$$
\widehat {\sigma} ^ {2} = \frac {1}{n - K} (y - X \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}) ^ {\prime} (y - X \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}).\tag{4.7}
$$

To account for the parameters’ estimation risk and to incorporate prior information, regression estimation can be cast in a Bayesian setting. Our earlier discussion of prior elicitation applies with full force here. We consider two prior scenarios: a diffuse improper prior and an informative conjugate prior for the regression parameter vector, $( ( \beta , \sigma ^ { 2 } ) )$ 

Diffuse Improper Prior The joint diffuse improper prior for $\beta$ and $\sigma ^ { 2 }$ is given by 

$$
\pi (\boldsymbol {\beta}, \sigma^ {2}) \propto \frac {1}{\sigma^ {2}},\tag{4.8}
$$

where the regression coefficients can take any real value, $- \infty < \beta _ { k } < \infty ,$ for $k = 1 , \ldots , K ,$ and the disturbance variance is positive, $\sigma ^ { 2 } > 0 ,$ 

Combining the likelihood in (4.5) and the prior above, we obtain the posteriors of the model parameters as follows: 

■ The posterior distribution of $\beta$ conditional on $\sigma ^ { 2 }$ is (multivariate) normal:<sup>4</sup> 

$$
p \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} \mid y, X, \sigma^ {2}\right) = \mathrm{N} \left(\widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}, (X ^ {\prime} X) ^ {- 1} \sigma^ {2}\right),\tag{4.9}
$$

where $\widehat { \beta }$ is the OLS estimate in (4.6) and $( X ^ { \prime } X ) ^ { - 1 } \sigma ^ { 2 }$ is the covariance matrix of ${ \widehat { \beta } } .$ . 

■ The posterior distribution of $\sigma ^ { 2 }$ is inverted $\chi ^ { 2 }$ : 

$$
p \left(\sigma^ {2} \mid y, X\right) = \operatorname{Inv-} \chi^ {2} (n - K, \widehat {\sigma} ^ {2}),\tag{4.10}
$$

where ${ \widehat { \sigma } } ^ { 2 }$ is the estimator of $\sigma ^ { 2 }$ in (4.7). 

It could be useful to obtain the marginal (unconditional) distribution of $\beta$ in order to characterize it independently of $\sigma ^ { 2 }$ (as in practical applications, the variance is an unknown parameter).<sup>5</sup> It can be shown, by integrating the joint posterior distribution 

$$
p \left(\boldsymbol {\beta}, \sigma^ {2} \mid y, X\right) = p \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} \mid y, X, \sigma^ {2}\right) p \left(\sigma^ {2} \mid y, X\right)
$$

with respect to $\sigma ^ { 2 }$ , that $\beta ^ { \circ } s$ unconditional posterior distribution is a multi variate Student’s t-distribution with a kernel given by<sup>6</sup> 

$$
p \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} \mid \mathcal {Y}, \mathbf {X}\right) \propto \left((n - K) + (\boldsymbol {\beta} - \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}) ^ {\prime} \frac {X ^ {\prime} X}{\widehat {\sigma} ^ {2}} (\boldsymbol {\beta} - \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}})\right) ^ {- n / 2}.\tag{4.11}
$$

Notice that integrating $\sigma ^ { 2 }$ out makes $\beta ^ { \prime } s$ distribution more heavy-tailed, duly reflecting the uncertainty about $\sigma ^ { 2 } \bar { \mathbf { \zeta } } _ { \mathbf { S } }$ true value. Although $\beta ^ { \circ } s$ mean vector is unchanged, its variance increased (on average) by the term $\nu / ( \nu - 2 )$ ): 

$$
\pmb {\Sigma} _ {\beta} = \widehat {\sigma} ^ {2} (X ^ {\prime} X) ^ {- 1} \frac {\nu}{\nu - 2},
$$

where $\nu = n - K$ is the degrees of freedom parameter of the multivariate Student’s t-distribution. 

In conclusion of our discussion of the posteriors in the diffuse improper prior scenario, suppose we are interested particularly in one of the regression coefficients, say $\beta _ { k }$ . For example, $\beta _ { k }$ could be the return on a factor (size, value, momentum, etc.) in a multifactor model of stock returns. It can be shown that the standardized $\beta _ { k }$ has a Student’s t-distribution with $n - K$ degrees of freedom as its marginal posterior distribution, 

$$
\frac {\beta_ {k} - \widehat {\beta_ {k}}}{(b _ {k , k}) ^ {1 / 2}} | y, X \sim t _ {n - K},\tag{4.12}
$$

where $b _ { k , k }$ is the kth diagonal element of ${ \widehat { \sigma } } ^ { 2 } ( X ^ { \prime } X ) ^ { - 1 }$ and ${ \widehat { \beta } } _ { k }$ is the OLS estimate of $\beta _ { k }$ (the corresponding component of $\widehat { \beta } )$ . Bayesian intervals fo $\beta _ { k }$ can then be constructed analytically. 

Informative Prior Under the normality assumption for the regression residuals in (4.1), one can make use of the natural conjugate framework to reflect the existing prior knowledge and to obtain convenient analytical posterior results. Thus, let us assume that the regression coefficients vector, $\beta ,$ , has a normal prior distribution (conditional on $\sigma ^ { 2 } )$ and $\sigma ^ { 2 } - \mathrm { a n }$ inverted $\chi ^ { 2 }$ prior distribution: 

$$
\pmb {\beta} \mid \sigma \sim \mathrm{N} (\pmb {\beta} _ {0}, \sigma^ {2} \pmb {A})\tag{4.13}
$$

and 

$$
\sigma^ {2} \sim \operatorname{Inv-} \chi^ {2} \left(\nu_ {0}, c _ {0} ^ {2}\right).\tag{4.14}
$$

Four parameters have to be determined a priori: $\beta _ { 0 } , \ A , \ \nu _ { 0 } ,$ , and $c _ { 0 } ^ { 2 } .$ . The scale matrix A is often chosen to be $\tau ^ { - 1 } ( X ^ { \prime } \bar { X } ) ^ { - 1 }$ in order to obtain a prior covariance the same as the covariance matrix of the OLS estimator of $\beta$ up to a scaling constant. Varying the (scale) parameter, $\tau _ { : }$ , allows one to adjust the degree of confidence one has that $\beta ^ { \circ } s$ mean is $\beta _ { 0 } -$ the smaller the value of $\tau _ { \cdot }$ the greater the degree of uncertainty about $\beta$ . 

The easiest way to assert the prior mean, $\beta _ { 0 } ,$ , is to fix it at some default value (such as $^ { 0 , }$ depending on the estimation context), unless more specific prior information is available, or to set it equal to the OLS estimate, ${ \widehat { \beta } } .$ , obtained from running the regression (4.1) on a prior sample of data.<sup>7</sup> 

The parameters of the inverted $\chi ^ { 2 }$ distribution could be asserted using a prior sample of data as follows: 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \nu_ {0} = n _ {0} - K \\ c _ {0} ^ {2} = \frac {1}{\nu_ {0}} \big (\mathbf {y} _ {0} - \mathbf {X} _ {0} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {0} \big) ^ {\prime} \big (\mathbf {y} _ {0} - \mathbf {X} _ {0} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {0} \big). \end{array}
$$

where the subscript, 0, refers to the prior data sample. If no prior data sample is available, the inverted $\chi ^ { 2 }$ hyperparameters could be specified by expressing beliefs about the prior mean and variance of $\sigma ^ { 2 }$ , using the expressions in (3.28) in Chapter 3. 

The posterior distributions for the model parameters, $\beta$ and $\sigma ^ { 2 }$ have the same form as the prior distributions, however, their parameters are updated to reflect the data information, along with the prior beliefs. 

■ The posterior for $\beta$ is 

$$
p \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} \mid y, X, \sigma^ {2}\right) = N \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma} _ {\beta}\right),\tag{4.15}
$$

where the posterior mean and covariance matrix of $\beta$ are given by 

$$
\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} = \left(\boldsymbol {A} ^ {- 1} + \boldsymbol {X} ^ {\prime} \boldsymbol {X}\right) ^ {- 1} \left(\boldsymbol {A} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\beta} _ {0} + \boldsymbol {X} ^ {\prime} \boldsymbol {X} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}\right)\tag{4.16}
$$

and 

$$
\boldsymbol {\Sigma} _ {\beta} = \sigma^ {2} \left(\boldsymbol {A} ^ {- 1} + \boldsymbol {X} ^ {\prime} \boldsymbol {X}\right) ^ {- 1}.\tag{4.17}
$$

We can observe that the posterior mean is a weighted average of the prior mean and the OLS estimator of $\beta ,$ as noted earlier in the chapter as well. See Chapter 6 for more details on this shrinkage effect. 

■ The inverted $\chi ^ { 2 }$ posterior distribution of $\sigma ^ { 2 }$ is 

$$
p \left(\sigma^ {2} \mid y, X\right) = \operatorname{Inv-} \chi^ {2} \left(v ^ {*}, c ^ {2 ^ {*}}\right).\tag{4.18}
$$

The parameters of $\sigma ^ { 2 } \bar { \mathbf { \zeta } } _ { \mathbf { S } }$ posterior distribution are given by 

$$
\nu^ {*} = \nu_ {0} + n\tag{4.19}
$$

and 

$$
\nu^ {*} c ^ {2 ^ {*}} = (n - K) \widehat {\sigma} ^ {2} + (\boldsymbol {\beta} _ {0} - \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}) ^ {\prime} H (\boldsymbol {\beta} _ {0} - \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}) + \nu_ {0} c _ {0} ^ {2},\tag{4.20}
$$

where $H = \left( ( X ^ { \prime } X ) ^ { - 1 } + A \right) ^ { - 1 }$ 

As done earlier, we can derive the marginal posterior distribution of $\beta$ by integrating $\sigma ^ { 2 }$ out of the joint posterior distribution. We obtain again a multivariate Student’s t-distribution, $t ( t ( \nu ^ { * } , \beta ^ { * } , Q ) )$ 

$$
p \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} \mid \mathcal {Y}, X\right) \propto \left(\nu^ {*} + (\boldsymbol {\beta} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) ^ {\prime} Q (\boldsymbol {\beta} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*})\right) ^ {- \nu^ {*} / 2},\tag{4.21}
$$

where $Q = \left( A ^ { - 1 } + X ^ { \prime } X \right) / c ^ { 2 ^ { * } }$ 

The mean of $\beta$ remains the same, $\beta ^ { * }$ (as it is independent of $\sigma ^ { 2 } )$ , while its unconditional (with respect to $\sigma ^ { 2 } )$ covariance matrix can be calculated using (3.30) in Chapter 3. The marginal posterior distribution for a single regression coefficient, $\beta _ { k } .$ can be shown to be 

$$
\frac {\beta_ {k} - \beta_ {k} ^ {*}}{(q _ {k , k}) ^ {1 / 2}} | \mathbf {y}, \mathbf {X} \sim t _ {\nu_ {0} + n - K},\tag{4.22}
$$

where $q _ { k , k }$ is the kth diagonal element of $\mathbf { Q } ^ { - 1 }$ and $\beta _ { k } ^ { * }$ is the kth component of $\beta ^ { * }$ . 

Prediction Suppose that we would like to predict the dependent variable, Y, p steps ahead in time and denote by the $\textit { p } \times \textit { 1 }$ vector $\widetilde { y } =$ $( y _ { T + 1 } , y _ { T + 2 } , . . . , y _ { T + p } )$ these future observations. We assume that the future observations of the independent variables are known and given by $\widetilde { X }$ . Let us use (3.20) in Chapter 3 to express the predictive density in the linear regression context, 

$$
p (\widetilde {y} \mid y, \widetilde {X}, X) = \iint p (\widetilde {y} \mid \boldsymbol {\beta}, \sigma^ {2}, \widetilde {X}) p (\boldsymbol {\beta}, \sigma^ {2} \mid y, X) d \boldsymbol {\beta}, \sigma^ {2},\tag{4.23}
$$

where $\begin{array} { r } { p ( { \boldsymbol { \beta } } , \sigma ^ { 2 } | y , X ) } \end{array}$ is the joint posterior distribution of $\beta$ and $\sigma ^ { 2 }$ . 

It can be shown that the predictive distribution is multivariate Student’s t. Under the diffuse improper prior scenario, the predictive distribution is 

$$
p (\widetilde {y} \mid y, \widetilde {X}, X) = t (n - K, \widetilde {X} \widehat {\beta}, S),\tag{4.24}
$$

where $S = { \widehat \sigma } ^ { 2 } ( I _ { \mathit { p } } + \widetilde { X } ( X ^ { \prime } X ) ^ { - 1 } \widetilde { X } ^ { ' } )$ and $\widehat { \beta }$ is the posterior mean of $\beta$ under the    diffuse improper scenario. In the case of the informative prior, the predictive distribution of $\widetilde { y }$ is 

$$
p (\widetilde {y} \mid y, \widetilde {X}, X) = t (v _ {0} + n, \widetilde {X} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}, V),\tag{4.25}
$$

where $V = c ^ { 2 ^ { * } } ( I _ { \flat } + \widetilde { X } ( A ^ { - 1 } + X ^ { \prime } X ) ^ { - 1 } \widetilde { X } ^ { ' } )$ and $\beta ^ { * }$ is the posterior mean of $\beta$ in (4.16). 

Certainly, it is again possible to derive the distribution for the predictive distribution for a single component of $\widetilde { y } - a$ univariate Student’s t-distribution—in the two scenarios, respectively, 

$$
\frac {\widetilde {y _ {k}} - \widetilde {X ^ {k}} \widehat {\beta_ {k}}}{s _ {k , k} ^ {1 / 2}} \sim t _ {n - K},\tag{4.26}
$$

where $\widetilde { X ^ { k } }$ is the kth row of $\widetilde { X }$ (the observations of the independent variables pertaining to the kth future period), and $s _ { k , k }$ is the kth diagonal element of the scale matrix, S, in (4.24), and 

$$
\frac {\widetilde {y} _ {k} - \widetilde {X} ^ {k} \beta_ {k} ^ {*}}{\nu_ {k , k} ^ {1 / 2}} \sim t _ {\nu_ {0} + n - K},\tag{4.27}
$$

where $\nu _ { k , k }$ is the kth diagonal element of the scale matrix, V, in (4.25). 

The Case of Unequal Variances We mentioned earlier in the chapter that the equal-variance assumption in (4.3) might be somewhat restrictive. Two examples would help clarify what that means. First, suppose that the n observations of Y are collected through time. It is a common practice in statistical estimation to use the longest available data record, likely spanning many years. Changes in the underlying economic or financial paradigms, the way data are recorded, and so on, that might have occurred during the sample period might have caused the variance of the random variable (as well as its mean, for that matter) to shift.<sup>8</sup> The equal-variance assumption would then lead to variance overestimation in the low-variance period(s) and variance underestimation in the high-variance period(s). When the variance (and/or mean) shifts permanently, the so-called ‘‘structural-break’’ models can be employed to reflect it.<sup>9</sup> In Chapter 11, we discuss the so-called ‘‘regime-switching’’ models, in which parameters are allowed to change values according to the state of the world prevailing in a particular period in time. 

Second, if our estimation problem is based on observations recorded at a particular point in time (producing a cross-sectional sample), the equal-variance assumption might be violated again. All units in our sample could potentially have different variances, so that $\begin{array} { r } { \mathrm { v a r } ( y _ { i } ) = \sigma _ { i } ^ { 2 } . } \end{array}$ , instead of var $( y _ { i } ) = \sigma ^ { 2 }$ as in (4.3), for $i = 1 , \ldots ,$ n. Estimation would then be severely hampered because this would imply a greater number of unknown parameters (variances and regression coefficients) than available data points. 

In practice one would perhaps be able to identify groups of homoge neous sample units that can be assumed to have equal variances. Suppose, for instance, that the cross-sectional sample consists of small-cap and large-cap stock returns. One could then expect that the return variances (volatilities) across the two groups differ but assume that companies within each group have equal return volatilities. More generally, one could assume some form of functional relation among the unknown variances—this would serve to reduce the number of unknown parameters to estimate. We now provide one possible way to address the variance inequality in the case when the sample observations can be divided into two homogeneous (with respect to their variances) groups or when a structural break (whose timing we know) is present in the sample.<sup>10</sup> 

Denote the observations from the two groups by $\pmb { y } _ { 1 } = ( y _ { 1 , 1 } , y _ { 1 , 2 } , \dots , y _ { 1 , n _ { 1 } } )$ and $\pmb { y } _ { 2 } = ( y _ { 2 , 1 } , y _ { 2 , 2 } , . . . , y _ { 2 , n _ { 2 } } )$ , so that $y = ( y _ { 1 } , \ y _ { 2 } )$ and $n _ { 1 } + n _ { 2 } = n .$ . The univariate regression setup in (4.1) is modified as 

$$
\begin{array}{l} y _ {1} = X _ {1} \boldsymbol {\beta} + \epsilon_ {1} \\ y _ {2} = X _ {2} \boldsymbol {\beta} + \epsilon_ {2}, \end{array}\tag{4.28}
$$

where $X _ { 1 }$ and $X _ { 2 }$ are, respectively, $( n _ { 1 } ~ \times ~ K )$ and $( n _ { 2 } ~ \times ~ K )$ matrices of observations of the independent variables. The disturbances are assumed to be independent and distributed as 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \epsilon_ {1} \sim \mathrm{N} (0, \sigma_ {1} ^ {2} I _ {n _ {1}}) \\ \epsilon_ {2} \sim \mathrm{N} (0, \sigma_ {2} ^ {2} I _ {n _ {2}}), \end{array}\tag{4.29}
$$

where $\sigma _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } \neq \sigma _ { 2 } ^ { 2 }$ . The likelihood function for the model parameters, $\beta , \sigma _ { 1 } ^ { 2 }$ , and $\sigma _ { 2 } ^ { 2 }$ is given by 

$$
\begin{array}{l} L \left(\boldsymbol {\beta}, \sigma_ {1} ^ {2}, \sigma_ {2} ^ {2} \mid \mathcal {Y}, X _ {1}, X _ {2}\right) \propto (\sigma_ {1} ^ {2}) ^ {- \frac {n _ {1}}{2}} (\sigma_ {2} ^ {2}) ^ {- \frac {n _ {2}}{2}} \\ \qquad \times \exp \biggl (- \frac {1}{2 \sigma_ {1} ^ {2}} (\mathcal {Y} _ {1} - X _ {1} \boldsymbol {\beta}) ^ {\prime} (\mathcal {Y} _ {1} - X _ {1} \boldsymbol {\beta}) \\ \qquad - \frac {1}{2 \sigma_ {2} ^ {2}} (\mathcal {Y} _ {2} - X _ {2} \boldsymbol {\beta}) ^ {\prime} (\mathcal {Y} _ {2} - X _ {2} \boldsymbol {\beta}) \biggr). \end{array}\tag{4.30}
$$

A noninformative diffuse prior can be asserted, as in (3.5), by assuming that the parameters are independent. The prior is written, then, as 

$$
\pi (\boldsymbol {\beta}, \sigma_ {1}, \sigma_ {2}) \propto \frac {1}{\sigma_ {1} \sigma_ {2}}.
$$

It is straightforward to write out the joint posterior density of $\beta , \sigma _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } .$ and $\sigma _ { 2 } ^ { 2 }$ , which can be integrated with respect to the two variances to obtain the marginal posterior distribution of the regression coefficients vector. 

Zellner (1971) shows that the marginal posterior of $\beta$ is the product of two multivariate Student’s t-densities (not a surprising result, since the likelihood in (4.30) is the product of two normal likelihoods), 

$$
p (\boldsymbol {\beta} \mid y, X _ {1}, X _ {2}) \propto t \left(v _ {1}, \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {1}, S _ {1}\right) \times t \left(v _ {2}, \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {2}, S _ {2}\right),
$$

where, for $i = 1 , 2 , \widehat { \beta } _ { i }$ is the OLS estimator of $\beta$ in the two expressions in (4.28) viewed as separate regressions, 

$$
\nu_ {i} = n _ {i} - K, \qquad S _ {i} = \widehat {s _ {i} ^ {2}} (\boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {\prime} \boldsymbol {X} _ {i}),
$$

and 

$$
\widehat {s _ {i} ^ {2}} = \frac {1}{n _ {i} - K} (\mathbf {y} _ {i} - \mathbf {X} _ {i} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta} _ {i}}) ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {y} _ {i} - \mathbf {X} _ {i} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta} _ {i}}).
$$

Zellner shows that the marginal posterior of $\beta$ above can be approximated with a normal distribution (through a series of asymptotic expansions). 

We conclude this discussion with a brief comment on a related violation of the univariate regression assumptions outlined earlier in the chapter. When analyzing data collected through time, it is more likely than not that the data are serially correlated. That is, the assumption that the regression disturbances are independent is violated. For example, dependence of returns through time might be caused by time-dependence of the return volatility (and/or the mean of returns). We discuss volatility modeling in Chapters 10, 11, and 12. 

## Illustration: The Univariate Linear Regression Model

We now illustrate the posterior and predictive inference in a univariate linear regression model. We restrict our attention to the diffuse noninformative prior and the informative prior discussed thus far in order to take advantage of their analytical convenience. In the next chapter, we show how to employ numerical computation to tackle inference when no analytical results are available. 

Our data consist of the monthly returns on 25 portfolios; the companies in each portfolio are ranked according to market capitalization and book-to-market (BM) ratios. (See Chapter 9 for further details on this data set.) The returns we use for model estimation span the period from January 1995 to December 2005 (a total of 132 time periods). We extract the factors that best explain the variability of returns of the 25 portfolios using principal components analysis. (See Chapter 14 for more details on multifactor models.) The first five factors explain around 95% of the variability and we use their returns as the independent variables in our linear regression model, making up the matrix X (the first column is a column of ones). The return on the portfolio consisting of the companies with the smallest size and BM ratios is the dependent variable y. In addition, returns recorded for the months from January 1990 to December 1994 (a total of 60 time periods) are employed to compute the hyperparameters of the informative prior distributions, in the manner explained in the previous section. Our interest centers primarily on the posterior inference for the regression coefficients, $\beta _ { k } , k = 1 , \ldots , 6 \cdot$ —the intercept and the five factor exposures (in the terminology of multifactor models). 

Posterior Distributions The prior and posterior parameter values for $\beta$ are given in Exhibit 4.1 Part A of the exhibit presents the results under the diffuse improper prior assumption and Part B under the informative prior assumption. In parentheses are the posterior standard deviations, computed using the expression in (3.26) in Chapter 3. The OLS estimates of the regression coefficients are, of course, given by the posterior means in the diffuse prior scenario. Notice how the posterior mean of $\beta$ under the informative prior is shrunk away from the OLS estimate and toward the prior value, for the chosen value of $\tau = 1$ . We could introduce more uncertainty into the prior distribution of $\beta$ (make it less informative) by choosing a smaller value of τ —the posterior mean of $\beta$ would then be closer to the OLS estimate. Conversely, the stronger our prior belief about the mean of $\beta _ { ; }$ the closer the posterior mean would be to the prior mean. 

Credible Intervals Since the marginal posterior distribution of $\beta _ { k } , \ k =$ $1 , \ldots , 6 ,$ , is of known form—Student’s t—we can compute analytically the Bayesian confidence intervals for the regression coefficients. We provide several quantiles from the distribution of each $\beta _ { k }$ . For example, under the diffuse improper prior, the 95% (symmetric) Bayesian interval for $\beta _ { 2 }$ is $( - 0 . 3 1 8 7 , \ - 0 . 3 0 2 9 )$ ), while, under the informative prior, the 99% (symmetric) Bayesian interval for $\beta _ { 6 }$ is $( - 0 . 0 1 6 2 , 0 . 1 1 8 0 )$ 11 

Hypothesis Comparison In the frequentist regression tradition, testing the significance of the regression coefficients is of great interest—the validity of the null hypothesis $\beta _ { k } = 0$ is examined. In the Bayesian setting, we could evaluate and compare the posterior probabilities, $P ( \beta _ { k } ) 0 \mid y , X )$ and $P ( \beta _ { k } < 0 | y , X )$ (given in Exhibit 4.1 for each factor exposure). We could safely conclude that the exposures on Factor 1 through Factor 4 are different from zero—the mass of their posterior distributions is concentrated on either positive or negative values. For the exposure on Factor 5, the picture is less than clear-cut. Under the diffuse, improper prior, a bit over $5 0 \%$ of the posterior mass is below zero and the rest—above zero. Therefore, one would perhaps take the pertinence of this factor for explaining the variability of the return on the small-cap/small-BM portfolio with a grain of salt. Notice, however, how the situation changes in the informative-prior case. More than 95% of the posterior mass is above zero. The strong prior beliefs about a positive mean of $\beta _ { 6 }$ lead to the conclusion that the exposure of the portfolio returns to Factor 5 is not zero. Exhibit 4.2 further illustrates these observations.
